XXI. 

We must look back on the Nazi terror in Europe as the most tragic period in Jewish history since the destruction of the Temple. In all the miseries of our people, in all the crises which they survived, none has matched the fearful destruction and murder of European Jewry by the Nazis.

In the age of wars and revolutions after 1914, the most striking fact was the rise of a new kind of government known as fascism. This was a military state controlling the industry, the education, the living and thinking of its people. It was called totalitarian, for all activity was under the domination of the state. The state was ruled by one party; the party was controlled by one man. The first great Fascist revolution was led by 

Benito Mussolini in Italy. It was followed by many similar governments in other countries. The most important of them all was the German government begun by Adolf Hitler. 

1. The Nazi Party. A turning point in modern history, and consequently in that of the Jews, was the rise to power, in January 1933, of Adolf Hitler, leader of the National Socialist OR 

German Workers' Party. Hitler's slogan was "Blood and Soil" -the German Aryan people on the German land. This involved the race theory, the beginnings of which we studied in Chapter XVI. Hitler taught the Germans that they were the "master race," that all other people were their inferiors, and that the 

Jews were the very lowest of all mankind.

He began a course of terrible oppression. All liberals, socialists, communists, and labor unions were outlawed. All who did not agree with the Nazis lost their jobs; many were locked up in concentration camps. But this same violence was used on the 281

Jews whether or not they opposed the Nazis simply because they were born Jews. The Nazis also attacked people of partial 

Jewish descent, and even Jews who had joined Christian churches, because they were of the so-called "Jewish race" and were therefore considered the enemies of all true Germans. But 

Hitler and his henchmen did not stop with the Jews of Germany. 

In world-wide propaganda they declared that all Jews were a cancerous growth on society and should be ruthlessly exterminated.

The destruction of 600,000 Germans Jews and 250,000 part- 

Jews ("non-Aryans") was rapid. Hitler's victims lost their positions; their businesses were boycotted and finally taken away; Jewish children were excluded from the public schools; 

Jewish, as well as radical, books were publicly burned. In 1935, the German citizenship of Jews, guaranteed in the constitution of the empire in 1871, was taken away, and under the Nuremberg Laws they became a lower class of non-citizens. They were excluded from every phase of German life; they were driven to the organization of their own Jewish schools, theaters, and clubs a complete return to ghetto life. Finally, they were made to wear a yellow badge, as they did in the Middle Ages. 

November 1938 brought two days of rioting all over Germany.

The pretext was the shooting of a German diplomat in Paris by a young Jew; actually every phase of the violence had been arranged in advance. Practically every synagogue in Germany was looted, then burned to the ground. Most of the men were taken to concentration camps; many of them were killed. The property of Jews had been carefully registered in advance to make it easier for the Germans to collect from them a fine of a billion marks. This wiped out almost everything they had been able to save after the years of poverty and oppression. 

By this time many Jews had managed to escape from Germany. Some had fled to America, and others to England or 

Palestine, where they were safe. The League of Nations ap-

pointed a Committee for Refugees, headed by an American, James G. McDonald. But he resigned after a time, declaring that the most pressing need was not to aid refugees, but to stop the Germans from creating more. In 1938, President Franklin 

D. Roosevelt called a meeting of thirty nations in Evian, France; they formed an Intergovernmental Committee, but did not succeed in finding places where great numbers of refugees might find permanent homes. When, in 1943, British and American representatives met in Bermuda, it was decided that rescue would have to be postponed until after the war! 

The refugees scattered over the face of the earth. The largest number went to Palestine. Others found refuge in North or 

South America, although every nation had immigration laws which made it very difficult to enter. When the St. Louis brought 

900 refugees to Cuba, it was found that their papers were not in order. For months the ship roamed the seas from port to port, until several countries at last took pity on the wanderers and opened their doors. Thousands of refugees crowded into faraway Shanghai, China, because there no visas were demanded; they were shortly sucked into another whirlpool when the Japanese war against China broke out. 

Other Jews thought they were safe in France, Czechoslovakia, or elsewhere in Europe; but the Second World War began in 

September 1939 and the Nazis followed the fugitives everywhere in Europe. Everywhere the brown-clad conquerors came they oppressed and enslaved; everywhere they deliberately destroyed the Jews.

Among those who escaped to America was Albert Einstein, the physicist; he has since served his adopted country well, blazing the trail for the making of the atom bomb. Sigmund 

Freud, the world-famous psychologist, was an aged man living in Vienna when the Nazis entered; he was forced to pay ransom before he left for England. There he died shortly afterward, but his daughter Anna carried on his work by caring for and

Sloa

Kocha curing child-victims of the bombings. Henri Bergson, the 

French philosopher, was exempted from Nazi persecution as an honored member of the French Academy, but the old man stood in line to register as a Jew. Leon Blum, the French- 

Jewish leader of the Socialist party and ex-premier of France, was imprisoned by that country's puppet government and tried for treason; but he succeeded in defending himself so eloquently that even the corrupt court dared not condemn him to death. 

Another who miraculously survived was Rabbi Leo Baeck, chief rabbi of Berlin; he had refused to leave his people, although given an opportunity to escape, and spent the war years in the concentration camp at Teresienstadt.

The Poles and the Russians, the Germans had decided, might be spared to work for their Nazi masters in a sort of halfslavery; but the Jews must be exterminated to the last helpless child. It was their boast that soon Europe would no longer be contaminated with Jews. So many and so shocking were the 

Nazi crimes that the defeated butchers were tried by an international court for the first time in history. When the war ended in 1945 the Nazi leaders at Nuremberg were confronted with their "crimes against humanity." Among their other atrocities they were held guilty of the murder of millions of helpless Jews. 

2. The Results of the War. During four years the Nazis had controlled all Europe from the Baltic Sea to the Mediterranean, from the Atlantic to the Volga. This vast area had included about eight million Jews, and centuries-old centers of Jewish life and culture. The largest number of Jews lived in 

Poland; there were also great numbers in the Ukraine, Hungary, Roumania, and other countries in eastern Europe, as well as important bodies of Jews in France and other nations to the west of Germany.

Now the determined effort to destroy them completely had, in great part, succeeded. Fully six million Jews died during those terrible years, not only because of the war, starvation, and

disease, but also in actual massacre. Jews were machine-gunned in the captured cities of Poland and Russia. French, Austrian, and Dutch Jews were searched out in relentless man-hunts to be shipped in cattle cars to the death camps of Maideneck and Auschwitz.

The conquered populations were held down by every form of organized and systematic violence. The Nazis set up labor camps where starved men and women who still had a little strength were compelled to work for them. In every conquered territory they set up concentration camps for all those suspected of rebellion or even leadership among their own people. Most horrible of all, they organized extermination camps to kill men, women, and children by the hundreds of thousands, with gas chambers for putting them to death and crematoriums for destroying the bodies.

After the war, a group of leading American editors and congressmen were invited by General Eisenhower to visit several of these camps. They were to report these horrors to Americans at home who felt that the accounts of Nazi barbarity had been exaggerated. These men saw starved bodies piled like cordwood and warehouses filled with the clothes and shoes of the victims. 

Now they understood why even in wartime the Germans had been comfortably dressed! Now they understood and repeated the words of many an Allied soldier: "If I had not seen all this with my own eyes, I would never have believed it."

The center of all this mass murder was Poland, where three million Jews had lived before the war. At first the Nazis organized a Jewish reservation near Lublin, to which they sent Jewish prisoners from the conquered lands. But because of the crowded and unsanitary conditions, an epidemic broke out and the reservation was abandoned. Next, in the larger Polish cities the conquerors organized ghettos, surrounded by great stone walls, where the Jews, rounded up from the towns and villages, were crowded like cattle. Finally these ghettos were emptied

into the death camps until there were no Jews left to suffer shame and torment.

The Warsaw ghetto was the largest; some half-million people were huddled into this small section of the city. One head of a community killed himself rather than prepare for the Germans lists of those to be sent to the gas chambers; he would not be a tool in helping to execute his fellow-Jews. Within the ghetto walls the Jews organized synagogues and schools, Zionist societies and clubs for Jewish culture; they also gathered what weapons they could from sympathizers in the liberal Polish underground.

In April, 1943, when the number of the inhabitants of the Warsaw ghetto had been reduced to about 40,000, the desperate remnant began their last and hopeless resistance. Jews who had never handled weapons before fired on the storm troopers who entered for their weekly quota of victims. For six weeks they desperately resisted the German army's tank and artillery attacks. The Germans destroyed the ghetto house by house, until the last of the heroes wrapped himself in the flag of Zion and leaped from the roof of a burning building to the blood-stained street below. Similar resistance flared up in other ghettos and sometimes even in the concentration camps. The descendants of the Maccabees and Bar Kochba proved themselves worthy of their fathers.

With all the methods used by the all-powerful conquerors, we wonder how a single Jew survived. For these broken survivors 

Edwin W. Pauley pleaded to President Truman in 1946: "We cannot bring millions of dead bodies to life-we cannot breathe the breath of life into the pits of human ash and human bones -we cannot even find the little bodies that once wore the thousands of baby shoes found amongst piles of shoes of every size. 

But we can take action-and take it now-to make certain that the over one million European Jews who survive the Nazi terror are given a chance to live."

3. Those Who Escaped. How did any manage to escape?

First, some German and Austrian Jews had fled to other lands before the war began. Many Polish Jews took refuge in Russia in 1939 at the outbreak of the war; in 1941 when the Germans attacked the Soviet Union, the Russian government sent great numbers of Jews, along with other civilians, east to safe areas. 

True, many thousands were left, but other thousands were saved. Other refugees, after incredible hardships and dangers, reached neutral countries like Switzerland, Sweden, and Spain. 

Considerable numbers of Jews, especially in Poland, the Ukraine, and France, joined the underground resistance. They hid in the hills and woods and carried on guerilla warfare against the Nazis. We know of one band of 600 Jewish underground fighters near Warsaw; there were countless others-civilians facing all the dangers of warfare. Some of them are alive today; their stories of underground activity have filled all who have listened to them with wonder and admiration.

A great many Jews were hidden by their Christian friends at the risk of their own lives. This was especially common in countries like France, Holland, and Belgium, where the people hated the Nazis. In Denmark, King Christian actually attended a synagogue to show his friendship for the Danish Jews; many heroic Danes smuggled Jews across the waters to Sweden, in the face of the Nazi demand for their surrender. In Italy, the 

Vatican flung open its gates to a number of Jewish refugees; others were sheltered in convents, Catholic orphanages, and private homes.

Not all the Nazi-controlled countries actually carried out the worst of the Nazi decrees. We have just mentioned Italy, where the racial laws had been passed by the Fascist government but were never strictly enforced. Roumania and Hungary had many more Jews; in fact, half the surviving Jews of Europe lived there. These countries had passed anti-Semitic laws which had driven the Jews out of many professions and into dire pov-

erty; but until the very end of the war, when the Nazis took complete control, there were no mass expulsions to the extermination camps.

Finally, when the Russian, British, and American armies liberated the concentration camps, some prisoners were still alive, though dying of starvation, disease, and the brutality of their jailers. In some cases, before they fled from the approaching armies, the storm troopers burned a camp and killed the inmates.

But the movement of the Allied troops was so rapid that many camps, such as Dachau and Buchenwald, were found intact with thousands of Jews, Poles, Czechs, and people of every nationality crowded in their filthy barracks; a few steps away, thousands of dead bodies were discovered, piled up and waiting for room in the crematoriums.

Altogether, at the close of the war there were about a million and a half Jews still alive in Europe, west of the Russian border. 

The Allied armies saved many of the concentration camp survivors with food and medical aid. The Jewish chaplains in the 

American and British armies were the first saviors. Imagine the feelings of the Jews—their families dead or scattered, they themselves imprisoned and tortured for no crime except their Jewish birth—when they saw American officers, wearing the tablets of the Law and the Shield of David as their Army insignia! 

Then came the United Nations Relief and Rehabilitation Ad ministration (UNRRA) and the Joint Distribution Committee 

(JDC) to reunite families, provide food and homes and the promise of a normal life.

Democratic countries such as France and Czechoslovakia welcomed back their Jewish citizens and provided refuge for many others. Children found in the homes of farmers or in Catholic schools were placed in Jewish institutions until they could be restored to their families or sent to Palestine. Some of the priests who had saved the lives of these Jewish children at the risk of their own wanted to keep them and bring them up as

Catholics; but many were eager to return them to their own people.

The Jews of eastern Europe did not want to return to the lands where they had faced discrimination and poverty; where, as in Poland, violence was again breaking out against them. 

These Jews, who had lost their homes and their families, wanted to leave Europe forever. The great majority longed to emigrate to Palestine, and President Truman proposed to the British government that a hundred thousand be given visas for that land at once. While the proposal was being considered, some were able to leave legally for various countries. Other hundreds managed to smuggle themselves into Palestine with the aid of the Palestine Brigade in the British Army and the citizens of the Homeland. 

4. World Jewry After the War. There were about 

11,000,000 Jews in the world in 1900; now, the number is again about the same. The great growth of our people for forty years has been cancelled by those destroyed in the war, the gas chambers, and the concentration camps. But the distribution and the life of the Jews who survived have been entirely changed. 

In 1900, most of the Jews of the world lived in eastern Europe. Here the oldest institutions of Jewish learning and culture were to be found; here were the centers of Jewish tradition and piety. The Jewish citizens of western Europe had advanced in wealth and modern education. Today few of the Jews of 

Germany and Austria survive; in France, two-thirds remain, making that group the largest Jewish body in western Europe. 

In the east, the great centers of Jewish life have been destroyed and it seems unlikely that many of them can ever be rebuilt. 

Hundreds of Jewish villages lie in ashes, their inhabitants dead or scattered. It has been estimated that in 1946 about 3,000,000 lived in the Soviet Union, and another 1,500,000 in all the rest of Europe.

With about 5,000,000 Jews-40 per cent of all the Jews in the

world now living in the United States, American Jewry has become the center of the life of our people. We have the numbers, the wealth, the influence. We have raised many millions of dollars to help our brothers abroad; and have taken the lead in the work for Palestine. In 1946 we collected $100,000,000, and in 1947 the goal was $170,000,000.

About 600,000 Jews now live in Palestine. This is a small number-only 5 per cent of the Jews in the whole world-but it is ten times as great as the Jewish population of Palestine during the First World War. This tiny land gave refuge to many who fled from Hitlerism. Jews are once again living a completely Jewish life in their ancient Homeland. 

Another 1,600,000 Jews are scattered the world over in Asia, Africa, South America, Australia. Over half a million live in the various countries of Latin America: sometimes a community of new refugees seeking a new free life, as in Mexico; sometimes a very large and old body facing new discrimination, as in Argentina. There are great groups of ancient origin in every large city of North Africa from Casablanca to Cairo; at the opposite end of the continent, in the Union of South Africa, there is a community of modern Jews, helping in the work of Zionism.

We have seen how the balance of world Jewry has been changed through the war, not only by the loss of numbers, the misery of the refugees, but even more by the new leadership of Jews in other parts of the world. Whether or not the Jews of 

Europe return to their native lands and reorganize their lives there, they will never again lead world Jewry as they did during the last four hundred years. Today the center of world Jewry is in America, with its hope and inspiration in Palestine.

XXII. 

UNFINISHED CHAPTERS 

The long and fruitful life of the Jews of western and central Europe is shattered. We cannot tell whether it will ever revive and become rich and productive again. We know only that today, at the close of the war, the Jewry of Europe consists of scattered groups of people who have been weakened and degraded. Most of them wish to leave the slaughterhouse forever and find homes in new lands. But where can these masses go? 

Now we want to visit the most important groups of Jews in other lands, to learn what their situation is today, and what they dare hope for in the future. We shall see at once that the Jewish situation is a part of the world situation, that the lives of Jews in any country are a part of the national life. The Jews of Germany became citizens under the empire. Under Hitler they were massacred, not because of any wrongdoing, but because their country had turned Fascist. The Jews of Russia were oppressed by the Czars but freed by the Revolution, merely because the government had changed and with it, its attitude toward minorities. But within the framework of national life, the Jews always have their own special problems, as we shall see in the case of Russia, America, and Palestine. 

1. The Jews of the Soviet Union. In Chapter XIX you read of the Russian Revolution. By 1941, when Russia was attacked by Germany, the country had grown strong and risen out of the poverty of czarist days, though it had not become as prosperous as America or England. The Jews had dropped out of their old occupations as traders and had been absorbed into farming and factory work. 292

UNFINISHED CHAPTERS 

293 Jewish collective farms increased in number, especially in the Crimea and the Ukraine. In 1934, the Soviet government began a project for Jewish settlement in Biro-Bidjan in eastern Siberia. About 10,000 Jews settled there, with Yiddish as the official language, and with every encouragement to develop a Jewish republic, a state in the Soviet Union. The growth of this pioneer project was slow and difficult before the war; during the war it could not grow at all.

For the next four years every bit of energy of every citizen of Russia was devoted to the national task of defeating the Nazis. The Jews of Russia carried their full share in the army, the guerilla bands, and the munition factories. In 1944, it was announced that 30,000 Russian Jews had received decorations for military services; Jews had even become generals in the Red Army.

At the same time, Russia tried to save as many Jews as pos sible from the Nazis. At least a quarter of a million Polish Jews found refuge in the Soviet Union. Millions of Jews had lived in western Russia, in cities like Kiev and Odessa which were destroyed by the Germans. The government made every effort to save them from the Nazis by sending them farther east to the Urals and Siberia. But the Nazi armies moved so swiftly that large numbers of civilians were destroyed with the cities, the factories, the farms throughout western Russia. Those civilians who were evacuated suffered great hardships, losing their homes and everything they possessed. But once established in a safer section, they toiled heroically to help defeat Hitler. 

Russia underwent another change during the war. Since the 

Revolution the government had disapproved of religious practices, as well as Zionism, although a few churches and synagogues remained open for those who wished to worship. Now religion was once more officially recognized. Russian rabbis broadcast appeals to the Jews of America and Palestine to aid the nation which was fighting so bravely against the Nazis. A

delegation of Russian Jews visited the United States, Canada, and Britain to appeal for friendship and help. Contacts were established with Palestine, whose Jews sent ambulances and medical supplies. The Jews of Russia, cut off from those of other lands for twenty-five years, were once again an active part of World Jewry!

We cannot say what the future of the Russian Jews may be. 

They have become thoroughly assimilated as members of a new social system. They are living as members of a different world, a new experience for them and for many other nationalities of the Soviet Union. Now that the ban on religion has been lifted, will the majority of the Russian Jews return to Judaism? Now that they have absorbed such large numbers from Jewish religious and cultural centers such as Warsaw and Vilna, will they be reborn through the loyalty and intense Judaism which once generated light for all Jews from Austria to America? Or will they continue merely as one of the recognized minorities of the Soviet Union, speaking the Yiddish language, fostering Yiddish schools, reading Yiddish books? At present, it is hard to predict whether Judaism will grow in a land with such a radically different way of life. 

2. American Jews and the War. Every world event since the rise of Hitler has been reflected in Jewish life in America. 

First came the work for the refugees, who were beginning to flee Germany in 1933. The Joint Distribution Committee had been planning to end its work about that time, after having raised and distributed staggering sums of money in every part of the world since 1914. Then the United Jewish Appeal, which included the Joint Distribution Committee, the United Palestine Appeal, and the National Refugee Service, took over and gathered funds on a larger scale than ever before. 

In 1946, the amazing goal of the United Jewish Appeal was $100,000,000; Christians as well as Jews gave generously; wanderers without homes started life anew in strange countries: all

UNFINISHED CHAPTERS 

295 were to benefit. The JDC now has workers in fifty countries all over the world to administer these funds and help the survivors of the war, and HIAS, Hebrew Immigrant Aid Society, has the tremendous task of directing and aiding immigrants from one land to another.

At the same time, Zionist sentiment in America has seen tremendous growth. This is most important, for American Jewry must take the lead among the Jewish people of the world. There were mass meetings and appeals to the government. The Biltmore Platform, demanding that Palestine be established as a Jewish Commonwealth, was adopted by the Zionist Organization of America in 1942. In the presidential campaign of 1944, both parties had planks favoring Zionism, and in December 1945 a resolution on behalf of Zionism was adopted by Congress. 

After America entered the Second World War, about 550,000 Jewish men and women served in the Army, Navy and Marine Corps; of these, casualties were suffered by 38,000 of whom 

11,000 were killed; 35,000 received awards and decorations.

There were 22 Jewish generals and admirals in the armed forces of this country. For the first time in our long history, a large number of rabbis went to war; there were 311 Jewish chaplains, rabbis who served the men in uniform in training camp and hospital, beachhead and battlefield. The Jewish Welfare Board was a part of the USO, which brought religion and recreation to the troops; it recommended chaplains to the Army and Navy. 

It distributed millions of bibles and prayer books; it brought 

Passover and Rosh Hashonah to Jewish soldiers and sailors wherever they might be.

The Jews at home bought war bonds, donated blood through the Red Cross, organized drives for wartime necessities, served as air raid wardens, nurses' aides, USO hostesses, and worked on every other patriotic project. Some of the scientists who developed the atom bomb were American Jews. 

One result of Nazi propaganda was the growth of anti-

Semitism in America. This was a first objective of the Nazis and their sympathizers in every country. Here they worked through the German-American Bund, the Silver Shirts, and various other bodies of Americans who accepted Hitler's teachings. Even some members of Congress spoke against the Jews; but several of them found themselves defeated in the election that soon followed. Many small Fascist groups sprang up to oppose President Roosevelt and the war, and at the same time to attack the Jews. Father Charles E. Coughlin, a Detroit priest who spoke on a national hookup every Sunday, published an inflammatory magazine, and sponsored the Fascist Christian Front, was probably the most dangerous agitator in these troubled times. 

But after Pearl Harbor most of these open attacks on fellow Americans were suppressed by the government. At the same time, the atrocities of the Nazis throughout Europe horrified the majority of Americans. They came to see that anti-Semitism was not simply a matter of personal feeling, but a part of Nazi propaganda to divide the American people. National church organizations published resolutions of protest. Other agencies worked for good will, for fair dealing, for a broader basis of understanding. The Fair Employment Practices Commission 

(FEPC) was established by President Roosevelt during the war to protect members of all minority groups in their rights to employment. This federal body expired in 1946, but several states, New York especially, have set up similar organizations. 

It is heartening that during the period of Nazi propaganda in America a large number of Jews won distinction in public life. This was partly due to the liberal movement led by Franklin D. Roosevelt and to that great president's breadth of view.

He appointed Henry Morgenthau, Jr., as Secretary of the 

Treasury, the second Jew to be a member of the Cabinet. At one time two Jews were members of the Supreme Court, Justices Louis D. Brandeis, appointed by President Wilson, and

UNFINISHED CHAPTERS 

297 

Benjamin Cardoza, appointed by President Hoover. But Jus tice Cardoza died in 1938, and the aged Justice Brandeis retired the following year. President Roosevelt then appointed

Felix Frankfurter to the Court. 

Bernard M. Baruch, adviser to President Wilson in the First

World War, held several important posts during the second global struggle, the most noteworthy being United States representative on the United Nations Commission on Atomic 

Energy. Sidney Hillman, the most outstanding Jew in the labor movement since Samuel Gompers, was appointed to the War Labor Board and other national agencies; he organized the 

Political Action Committee of the CIO in 1944. 

One great trend in American Jewry has been toward the unity needed not only to raise funds for relief but to defend ourselves against our enemies. In 1943 a new organization, the 

American Jewish Conference, came into being. This gathering of 300 delegates, representing the great national Jewish bodies of all types as well as many local communities, was concerned with the welfare of Jews all over the world. Everybody agreed on relief for the refugees; but when an overwhelming majority passed a strong resolution in favor of the Jewish Commonwealth of Palestine, some bodies withdrew from the Conference. 

However, this organization remains the nearest to a unified body that American Jewry has yet achieved. 

3. Growth and Struggle in Palestine. Through the depths of Nazi persecution, Palestine became the only hope for vast numbers of Jews. It was the only country which actually wanted immigrants. The newcomers brought their will to labor, their knowledge in science and the arts, whatever wealth they had managed to save from the oppressors. During the last eighteen years the Jewish population of Palestine has grown from 150,000 to 600,000; many of these immigrants were refugees. 

Some of the leading scholars and medical men of Germany and Austria found a place on the staff of the Hebrew Univer、

sity and its new hospital. Manufacturers from Vienna and 

Berlin opened factories. All were eager to work, to learn the 

Hebrew language, to live in a Jewish land. In 1946, the Jews now constituted one-third of the population of Palestine; in 

1920 they were only one-tenth. 

But this growth has been difficult! Palestine itself had a strict quota law, with the exact number of entrance visas arranged six months in advance. The British government grew cruel in applying these immigration laws. Some of the immigrants without visas were sent to detention camps; others were shipped to 

Mauritius, an island in the Indian Ocean. One ship, the Patria, was blown up in Haifa harbor in 1940, probably by some desperate refugee who could not bear to be turned away, and 250 people were killed. Another ship, the Struma, although not seaworthy, was forced to leave Constantinople because the proper visas were not granted by the English government; it sank in the Black Sea with 800 homeless souls.

But quota immigrants continued to arrive. Some, outside the quota, were admitted provisionally, while others were smuggled in secretly and hidden by devoted fellow-Jews. Perhaps the most inspiring chapter in the story of the refugees is the achievement of Youth Aliyah which brought 12,000 children to Palestine. Through this movement, Henrietta Szold became the 

Mother not only of the children of Palestine but also of the hunted waifs of the Second World War. Even before the first terrified migrations from the Hitler terror, she visited Germany to make plans to bring the young people of that unhappy country to Palestine. The movement grew as the storm troopers marched over Europe. Miss Szold raised the huge sums needed for the venture; she was first in the plans for new homes and education for the newcomers. Before she died, at the age of eighty-three, many of the "graduates of Youth Aliyah" had enlisted to fight the enemy who had driven them from their fatherland. 

The chief difficulty in building Palestine has always been po-

UNFINISHED CHAPTERS 

299 litical. The majority of the people were not Jews, but Arabs; 

Great Britain ruled both as a mandatory power for the League of Nations. Many of the Arabs, especially the rich and powerful landowners, did not want the Jews to bring in modern ideas. 

They wanted to run the country themselves in the old way, no matter how poor and ignorant they kept the mass of their people. The Arab leader, the Mufti (or religious head) of Jerusalem, plotted against the Jews and the British, took bribes from Mussolini and Hitler, and organized bloody riots against the Jewish settlers. The worst of these occurred from 1936 to . 

1939; the Jews defended themselves manfully, but many were killed, and a good proportion of their precious young trees and crops was wantonly destroyed.

When the war broke out in 1939, the Mufti sought safety in Berlin with his friend Hitler. For a long time it seemed that 

Palestine might be invaded; it was bombed more than once. 

Although they could not be drafted, many Jews volunteered to fight the Nazis. About 25,000 of them served in the British army in North Africa, Sicily, and Italy. At first, these Jews were placed in labor units, hauling supplies to the front. Finally, in 1945, a Jewish Brigade was organized in the British army, a fighting unit which played a part in the break-through in Italy. 

Wherever these Jewish soldiers went they sought out their fellow-Jews, fed them, established schools for their children, inspired them with news from their Jewish Homeland. As 

Jewish patriots, they fought the Nazis and rescued their victims. 

Meanwhile the Jews of Palestine raised food for the army, made munitions, established a home guard-all to help win the war against fascism.

Britain wavered between the demands of the Jews, who were allies in the war, and the threats of the Arabs, who were lukewarm and sometimes even hostile. In 1939, the British had issued the White Paper, which limited the purchase of land by Jews, set the number of Jewish immigrants at 75,000 for the

next five years, with further migration to be discussed with the Arabs; and promised the Arabs that the Jews would always remain a minority in the Homeland.

After the war came the desperate need of the released Jewish refugees. But now the British government, in accordance with the White Paper, declared further immigration illegal. President Truman urged that because of their desperate need, 100,000 refugees be admitted. Britain's answer was the appointment of a joint Anglo-American Committee in 1945 to investigate the condition of the Jews of Europe and Palestine. This committee approved President Truman's recommendation, even though it did not favor a Jewish Commonwealth. But their decision was ignored and a subsequent joint committee came out in favor of a partition scheme which neither Zionists nor Arabs were prepared to accept, and to which the American government objected.

All this political uncertainty, as well as the desperate need of the refugees, brought violence. The self-defense organization of Jews, known as Haganah, had kept a strict policy of restraint during the riots; the Jews merely defended themselves and refrained from attack. During the war, when an enemy invasion was feared, the Jewish settlers had been supplied with some weapons. Now they began to use these weapons to defend 

Jewish rights and to help the refugees to land. Meanwhile, the 

British stopped the overcrowded little ships off shore; removed the passengers, often ill and suffering from lack of food and water; and took them to the Island of Cyprus to wait for the political settlement that would decide their future. 

Not all the Palestinian Jews could be satisfied with endless debates over such a situation. Two groups in particular showed armed resistance. The Haganah blew up railroads, bridges, and radio stations, and tried in every way to harass the British army. But the Irgun Zvai Leumi, another secret group, resorted to direct attack. A particularly shocking exploit was an explosion

UNFINISHED CHAPTERS 

301 in the King David Hotel in Jerusalem, the British military headquarters, where many officers and civilian workers lost their lives. A much smaller body, the Stern group, tried to assassinate British leaders; two of its number killed Lord Moyne, a high British official.

As a result, the British imposed a curfew, searched cities and villages for hidden arms, and confined many Jews and their leaders in camps behind barbed wire. They did not torture or kill them, as the Nazis had, but they also tried repression by force.

As we write these lines the dreary debate still goes on. We can only hope that the dream of Herzl will soon be a reality, that the Jews of Palestine will evolve a government that will be a model for all the nations of the earth. For their builders dreamed and suffered and overcame that "the Law shall go forth from Zion and the Word of the Lord from Jerusalem." 

Freed from political disturbances, Palestine will surely grow into a center of Jewish learning and Jewish culture. Hebrew poetry and drama, Jewish art and music will become more Jewish, not because the writers and artists and musicians are Jews, but because they will be living and working in Jewish surroundings. The Chalutzim as they work in the fields do not sing of the glories of Israel's past; they exult in the new society they are rebuilding in the ancient land of their fathers. 

4. The United Nations. The United Nations today is the hope of the world, and consequently of the Jews, who are involved in all of the world's problems. This was evident in San 

Francisco, where the United Nations was organized in 1945. At that time, American Jewish organizations sent their representatives to place the needs of Israel before the delegates. 

They succeeded in part; they did not win approval for Zionism, which did not come up officially at the meeting, but they prevented endorsement of the anti-Zionist schemes brought up by the representatives of the Arab states. An International Bill

of Rights, that would protect people of all nationalities and religions, was supported by many churches, labor unions, and liberal organizations, which felt that the Bill of Rights in the American Constitution ought to be extended to the whole world. 

Such a Bill of Rights was actually proposed by the government of the United States and mentioned in the Charter of the United Nations. The Economic and Social Council of this body was instructed to draft the bill and see that it was carried out. 

Everybody knows that there is a wide gap between the adoption of a law and its enforcement, especially when it involves so many different countries and so many difficult problems. But the law is the first step, and as the United Nations becomes a strong working instrument, we can look forward to the protection of Jewish rights, with those of all other peoples, by a body much more powerful than any backward nation or any single tyrant. 

5. Will the Future Repeat the Past? The problems that confront the Jews today in Europe, in America, and in Palestine are not entirely new to us. We have faced nearly every one of them before during our long wanderings from Sinai. The Jew survived the horrors of medieval Europe; now again the remnant must build a new life on the ruins of the old. In Russia our people seem ready to fit themselves into a new scheme of life. The rebuilding of Palestine is a tremendous task; so was it in the day of Nehemiah, but the men of the Great Synagogue refashioned a finer and truer Judaism. 

In America the Jew again discovers a page that reads like the older chapters in his history. The Jews of Egypt under 

Greek rule, and later under the Moslems of Spain, although influenced by their kindly rulers, still remained Jews. In Egypt 

Philo taught and wrote; the later period produced a Maimonides. 

May history repeat itself in America! Time passes, conditions change, but the Jew endures.

We can no longer follow the Wanderer in his journey. Let us look our last upon him, an old man, white-haired but vigor-

UNFINISHED CHAPTERS 

303 ous, a traveler's staff in his hand. He has seen many cities rise to power and sink into decay; he has sat at the tables of beggars and of princes; he is very ancient and yet he has the strength of youth. For still clasped to his heart is the Book of his fathers, and in its pages is eternal life. Perhaps his wanderings will soon be over. But the message of his Book must continue to echo through the world, the message of his prophets, the call to worship and obey a righteous God. It is this ideal which has, given him eternal life. For the prophetic ideal of a better world is eternal truth and so it can never die.
